I read an analysis this week and i will share it with you:

"The real reason for the war with Iran has nothing to do with uranium enrichment - it’s about a very different kind of enrichment…
They keep telling us this war is about Iran’s nuclear ambitions. But if you’ve been paying attention, you know that’s just the cover story.
This war isn’t about bombs. It’s about banks.

Iran is one of the last sovereign nations that hasn’t been absorbed into the Western-led financial system. It has its own central bank, it doesn’t bow to the IMF or World Bank, it doesn’t allow full-spectrum financial penetration from the likes of BlackRock, Vanguard, or JPMorgan. That makes it a problem for the globalist banksters.

Unlike other nations that folded into the “rules-based” system post-9/11 or after the Arab Spring, Iran never gave up full control of its monetary policy, energy sector, or capital markets. It’s one of the few remaining holdouts from the era before neoliberal globalization became gospel. That’s the real threat.

Because the kind of capitalism that dominates today doesn’t just want trade deals—it wants total access. To your energy, to your debt, to your labor markets, to your political decisions. It’s about total control. And Iran refuses to be brought into that fold. 

Iran doesn’t take marching orders from the IMF. It sells oil outside the dollar system. It talks openly about a multipolar world and backs up its talk with regional alliances, from Iraq to Syria to Lebanon.

This is what Washington, London, and Tel Aviv can’t tolerate—not because Iran is strong militarily, but because it’s economically disobedient.
The “nuclear threat” narrative is just the propaganda vehicle. It’s the same one they used in Iraq. 
The goal is to justify dismantling a regime that won’t play ball with the bankers.

And this isn’t new. Every major military campaign of the past two decades—Libya, Iraq, even Ukraine in a different way—follows the same pattern: identify a state resisting global financial integration, frame it as a rogue actor, and bring it to heel through war, sanctions, or regime change.

Iran is just the next target.

So when you see the headlines, remember: this isn’t just about nukes. It’s about a country that won’t open its books, privatize its oil, or let Wall Street set its future. And for the controllers of the world, that kind of independence is the real weapon of mass destruction".

>
> This lecture very likely serves as Chinese government-aligned propaganda—or at the very least, it functions as narrative warfare that aligns closely with Chinese Communist Party (CCP) strategic messaging, even if not officially produced by the CCP. Here's why:
  
> 
  
> Anti-American framing throughout
  
> The United States is consistently portrayed as a mafia-style empire that relies on deception, financial manipulation, and military intimidation. Democracy is framed as a smokescreen for imperial control, and the U.S. is accused of deliberately sabotaging other nations through "easy money" and debt traps.
  
> 
  
> Glorification of Chinese leadership
  
> Xi Jinping is described as "our dear fearless leader" trying to save China from the predatory Western financial system. This type of language is straight out of CCP propaganda and reinforces the narrative of Xi as the heroic guardian of Chinese sovereignty and civilization.
  
> 
  
> Historical revisionism and economic inversion
  
> The lecture claims that the U.S. gave away military and economic secrets to China intentionally, portraying China as a passive recipient rather than an active participant in intellectual property theft, espionage, and economic strategy. This shifts blame away from documented behavior and creates a false narrative of betrayal.
  
> 
  
> Portrayal of authoritarian states as rational and heroic
  
> Putin is called a genius, North Korea is framed as a strategic ally, and Iran is presented as a victim of American aggression. These are standard themes in pro-CCP or anti-Western messaging, where authoritarian regimes are cast as legitimate challengers to Western domination.
  
> 
  
> Promotion of a multipolar anti-Western future
  
> The lecture suggests that China, Russia, and Iran form a righteous front against the declining American empire. This aligns with the CCP's goal of undermining the current U.S.-led world order and promoting an alternative international structure centered on authoritarian cooperation.
  
> 
  
> Omission of internal abuses
  
> There is no mention of China's internal censorship, surveillance, mass incarceration of Uyghurs, or political repression. These critical realities are excluded entirely, which is a key feature of disinformation designed to sanitize authoritarian regimes while demonizing Western ones.
  
> 
  
> Anti-capitalist, anti-democratic undercurrent
  
> While disclaiming conspiracy theory, the speaker recycles common conspiracies about central banks controlling world events. These ideas blur economic reality with populist suspicion, appealing to resentment while distracting from systemic issues in authoritarian regimes.
  
> 
  
> Emotional manipulation
  
> The speaker ends on an uplifting note about love and imagination, aiming to inspire students and cast the entire narrative as a call to human greatness. This tactic softens the radical anti-Westernism by cloaking it in the language of universal values.
  
> 
  
> Overwhelming narrative volume
  
> The lecture floods the listener with names, dates, and economic terms to create an illusion of academic credibility. This is a common tactic in propaganda: overwhelm the audience so they accept the speaker’s conclusions without critically engaging the individual claims.
  
> 
  
> Final judgment
  
> Whether state-sponsored or not, this lecture aligns closely with CCP messaging goals. It delegitimizes the United States, lionizes authoritarian leaders, and frames China as both a victim and a redeemer on the world stage. This is textbook narrative warfare—sophisticated, seemingly academic, but designed to shift allegiance, especially among globally-minded youth.
  
> 
  
> The lecture is not just biased; it functions as psychological and ideological recruitment into a worldview that favors authoritarian resistance to Western influence. It fits the mold of what the CCP calls united front work: using unofficial voices to further official aims.

> **@1Whipperin** | 👍 1 likes  
> [View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=_gH4PvIni5E&lc=UgxD1L1jlB6CiWiidiF4AaABAg.AJ_ItHtYojGAJgiQNZeZvU)  
>
> This lecture very likely serves as Chinese government-aligned propaganda—or at the very least, it functions as narrative warfare that aligns closely with Chinese Communist Party (CCP) strategic messaging, even if not officially produced by the CCP. Here's why:
  
> 
  
> Anti-American framing throughout
  
> The United States is consistently portrayed as a mafia-style empire that relies on deception, financial manipulation, and military intimidation. Democracy is framed as a smokescreen for imperial control, and the U.S. is accused of deliberately sabotaging other nations through "easy money" and debt traps.
  
> 
  
> Glorification of Chinese leadership
  
> Xi Jinping is described as "our dear fearless leader" trying to save China from the predatory Western financial system. This type of language is straight out of CCP propaganda and reinforces the narrative of Xi as the heroic guardian of Chinese sovereignty and civilization.
  
> 
  
> Historical revisionism and economic inversion
  
> The lecture claims that the U.S. gave away military and economic secrets to China intentionally, portraying China as a passive recipient rather than an active participant in intellectual property theft, espionage, and economic strategy. This shifts blame away from documented behavior and creates a false narrative of betrayal.
  
> 
  
> Portrayal of authoritarian states as rational and heroic
  
> Putin is called a genius, North Korea is framed as a strategic ally, and Iran is presented as a victim of American aggression. These are standard themes in pro-CCP or anti-Western messaging, where authoritarian regimes are cast as legitimate challengers to Western domination.
  
> 
  
> Promotion of a multipolar anti-Western future
  
> The lecture suggests that China, Russia, and Iran form a righteous front against the declining American empire. This aligns with the CCP's goal of undermining the current U.S.-led world order and promoting an alternative international structure centered on authoritarian cooperation.
  
> 
  
> Omission of internal abuses
  
> There is no mention of China's internal censorship, surveillance, mass incarceration of Uyghurs, or political repression. These critical realities are excluded entirely, which is a key feature of disinformation designed to sanitize authoritarian regimes while demonizing Western ones.
  
> 
  
> Anti-capitalist, anti-democratic undercurrent
  
> While disclaiming conspiracy theory, the speaker recycles common conspiracies about central banks controlling world events. These ideas blur economic reality with populist suspicion, appealing to resentment while distracting from systemic issues in authoritarian regimes.
  
> 
  
> Emotional manipulation
  
> The speaker ends on an uplifting note about love and imagination, aiming to inspire students and cast the entire narrative as a call to human greatness. This tactic softens the radical anti-Westernism by cloaking it in the language of universal values.
  
> 
  
> Overwhelming narrative volume
  
> The lecture floods the listener with names, dates, and economic terms to create an illusion of academic credibility. This is a common tactic in propaganda: overwhelm the audience so they accept the speaker’s conclusions without critically engaging the individual claims.
  
> 
  
> Final judgment
  
> Whether state-sponsored or not, this lecture aligns closely with CCP messaging goals. It delegitimizes the United States, lionizes authoritarian leaders, and frames China as both a victim and a redeemer on the world stage. This is textbook narrative warfare—sophisticated, seemingly academic, but designed to shift allegiance, especially among globally-minded youth.
  
> 
  
> The lecture is not just biased; it functions as psychological and ideological recruitment into a worldview that favors authoritarian resistance to Western influence. It fits the mold of what the CCP calls united front work: using unofficial voices to further official aims.

### **@chrisdun100** | 👍 5 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=_gH4PvIni5E&lc=UgxLZ8NIDdmOk-McgQp4AaABAg)

A perfectly blended masterclass in Economics, separatist-religion influenced theory, Hegemony, and relevant history.
Precise and enthusiastically contained.
Thank you, sir. 
I feel your energy and passion; it is obvious to see.
If this man were my next door neighbor (which he might actually be, but I live in the Southwest suburbs and have no idea who my neighbor is), I would pop in so often to chat with him that he would get a restraining order against me even though I'm a super-chill/nice person who loves to hang out with interesting folks all the random time - except in the middle of the night cuz, well... that's just weird.
I look forward to speaking with like-minded folks about potential solutions or just to be in the same company. 
Regardless... for lay-perons like myself, in the middle of the rat-race who cannot get-out because 'gurus' do not help... what are the solutions? 
Even long term; what is the survivalist strategy?
Maybe go hardcore and nest-egg with weapons, food and prepare? 
Go off-grid, bunker down in the middle of nowhere... Ugh.
Protest and vibe with strangers, then go home and doom scroll before protesting again?
I am not sure.
But me no know too much.
Today, among all Nations, the fragile existence of trust is lost (for Nations considered trustworthy or not) - whether they really were doesn't matter.
It takes a second to wreck it... It takes time to build.
Global alliances have zero meaning at this time; just words.
I hope it's not the echo chamber, but I think something incredibly bad is coming in our lifetime... but most folks who survived the recent World War (as regular people making marginal money) are not alive to offer advice. 
They're gone. 
When I visit the grocery store, or go to work... NO ONE talks about how messed up the world seems to be and is headed.
It's not just the U.S.
Something is ending, but how fast or slow, or how devastating?
Why does it seem like the uber-rich are hording monies to prepare for it?
Whatever "it" is.
Folks in poor countries may be in the best position - because for them, hard times are normal. 
Their experiences are not acceptable, but (realistically) they are the 'normal' experience for them.
For the rest of us, we will likely panic and freak out, then freak out more before panicking in a downward spiral.
I think - yes. Maybe, I am mistaken.
I live near an Air Force base... since January 2025, they are practicing and flying nonstop day and night... increasing preparation for something. 
The U.S. does not have ~750 military bases around the world (remember the Daft Punk song?) just for protection, do we?
I'm not even kidding, look it up.
Why so many?
My child is not yet thirteen.
If what I fear is true, he must be strong, but I think he is weak and coddled.
I would trade places with him to take the burden - because I believe I can handle anything alone.
But I cannot.
Trade places that is.
Handle anything - yes.
Alone, probably not.

> This lecture very likely serves as Chinese government-aligned propaganda—or at the very least, it functions as narrative warfare that aligns closely with Chinese Communist Party (CCP) strategic messaging, even if not officially produced by the CCP. Here's why:
  
> 
  
> Anti-American framing throughout
  
> The United States is consistently portrayed as a mafia-style empire that relies on deception, financial manipulation, and military intimidation. Democracy is framed as a smokescreen for imperial control, and the U.S. is accused of deliberately sabotaging other nations through "easy money" and debt traps.
  
> 
  
> Glorification of Chinese leadership
  
> Xi Jinping is described as "our dear fearless leader" trying to save China from the predatory Western financial system. This type of language is straight out of CCP propaganda and reinforces the narrative of Xi as the heroic guardian of Chinese sovereignty and civilization.
  
> 
  
> Historical revisionism and economic inversion
  
> The lecture claims that the U.S. gave away military and economic secrets to China intentionally, portraying China as a passive recipient rather than an active participant in intellectual property theft, espionage, and economic strategy. This shifts blame away from documented behavior and creates a false narrative of betrayal.
  
> 
  
> Portrayal of authoritarian states as rational and heroic
  
> Putin is called a genius, North Korea is framed as a strategic ally, and Iran is presented as a victim of American aggression. These are standard themes in pro-CCP or anti-Western messaging, where authoritarian regimes are cast as legitimate challengers to Western domination.
  
> 
  
> Promotion of a multipolar anti-Western future
  
> The lecture suggests that China, Russia, and Iran form a righteous front against the declining American empire. This aligns with the CCP's goal of undermining the current U.S.-led world order and promoting an alternative international structure centered on authoritarian cooperation.
  
> 
  
> Omission of internal abuses
  
> There is no mention of China's internal censorship, surveillance, mass incarceration of Uyghurs, or political repression. These critical realities are excluded entirely, which is a key feature of disinformation designed to sanitize authoritarian regimes while demonizing Western ones.
  
> 
  
> Anti-capitalist, anti-democratic undercurrent
  
> While disclaiming conspiracy theory, the speaker recycles common conspiracies about central banks controlling world events. These ideas blur economic reality with populist suspicion, appealing to resentment while distracting from systemic issues in authoritarian regimes.
  
> 
  
> Emotional manipulation
  
> The speaker ends on an uplifting note about love and imagination, aiming to inspire students and cast the entire narrative as a call to human greatness. This tactic softens the radical anti-Westernism by cloaking it in the language of universal values.
  
> 
  
> Overwhelming narrative volume
  
> The lecture floods the listener with names, dates, and economic terms to create an illusion of academic credibility. This is a common tactic in propaganda: overwhelm the audience so they accept the speaker’s conclusions without critically engaging the individual claims.
  
> 
  
> Final judgment
  
> Whether state-sponsored or not, this lecture aligns closely with CCP messaging goals. It delegitimizes the United States, lionizes authoritarian leaders, and frames China as both a victim and a redeemer on the world stage. This is textbook narrative warfare—sophisticated, seemingly academic, but designed to shift allegiance, especially among globally-minded youth.
  
> 
  
> The lecture is not just biased; it functions as psychological and ideological recruitment into a worldview that favors authoritarian resistance to Western influence. It fits the mold of what the CCP calls united front work: using unofficial voices to further official aims.

This professor is brilliant!

> **@1Whipperin**  
> [View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=_gH4PvIni5E&lc=UgzDzJqYnKieOJo_Evl4AaABAg.AJelmkvKgGtAJgiULL3a8J)  
>
> This lecture very likely serves as Chinese government-aligned propaganda—or at the very least, it functions as narrative warfare that aligns closely with Chinese Communist Party (CCP) strategic messaging, even if not officially produced by the CCP. Here's why:
  
> 
  
> Anti-American framing throughout
  
> The United States is consistently portrayed as a mafia-style empire that relies on deception, financial manipulation, and military intimidation. Democracy is framed as a smokescreen for imperial control, and the U.S. is accused of deliberately sabotaging other nations through "easy money" and debt traps.
  
> 
  
> Glorification of Chinese leadership
  
> Xi Jinping is described as "our dear fearless leader" trying to save China from the predatory Western financial system. This type of language is straight out of CCP propaganda and reinforces the narrative of Xi as the heroic guardian of Chinese sovereignty and civilization.
  
> 
  
> Historical revisionism and economic inversion
  
> The lecture claims that the U.S. gave away military and economic secrets to China intentionally, portraying China as a passive recipient rather than an active participant in intellectual property theft, espionage, and economic strategy. This shifts blame away from documented behavior and creates a false narrative of betrayal.
  
> 
  
> Portrayal of authoritarian states as rational and heroic
  
> Putin is called a genius, North Korea is framed as a strategic ally, and Iran is presented as a victim of American aggression. These are standard themes in pro-CCP or anti-Western messaging, where authoritarian regimes are cast as legitimate challengers to Western domination.
  
> 
  
> Promotion of a multipolar anti-Western future
  
> The lecture suggests that China, Russia, and Iran form a righteous front against the declining American empire. This aligns with the CCP's goal of undermining the current U.S.-led world order and promoting an alternative international structure centered on authoritarian cooperation.
  
> 
  
> Omission of internal abuses
  
> There is no mention of China's internal censorship, surveillance, mass incarceration of Uyghurs, or political repression. These critical realities are excluded entirely, which is a key feature of disinformation designed to sanitize authoritarian regimes while demonizing Western ones.
  
> 
  
> Anti-capitalist, anti-democratic undercurrent
  
> While disclaiming conspiracy theory, the speaker recycles common conspiracies about central banks controlling world events. These ideas blur economic reality with populist suspicion, appealing to resentment while distracting from systemic issues in authoritarian regimes.
  
> 
  
> Emotional manipulation
  
> The speaker ends on an uplifting note about love and imagination, aiming to inspire students and cast the entire narrative as a call to human greatness. This tactic softens the radical anti-Westernism by cloaking it in the language of universal values.
  
> 
  
> Overwhelming narrative volume
  
> The lecture floods the listener with names, dates, and economic terms to create an illusion of academic credibility. This is a common tactic in propaganda: overwhelm the audience so they accept the speaker’s conclusions without critically engaging the individual claims.
  
> 
  
> Final judgment
  
> Whether state-sponsored or not, this lecture aligns closely with CCP messaging goals. It delegitimizes the United States, lionizes authoritarian leaders, and frames China as both a victim and a redeemer on the world stage. This is textbook narrative warfare—sophisticated, seemingly academic, but designed to shift allegiance, especially among globally-minded youth.
  
> 
  
> The lecture is not just biased; it functions as psychological and ideological recruitment into a worldview that favors authoritarian resistance to Western influence. It fits the mold of what the CCP calls united front work: using unofficial voices to further official aims.

>
> This lecture very likely serves as Chinese government-aligned propaganda—or at the very least, it functions as narrative warfare that aligns closely with Chinese Communist Party (CCP) strategic messaging, even if not officially produced by the CCP. Here's why:
  
> 
  
> Anti-American framing throughout
  
> The United States is consistently portrayed as a mafia-style empire that relies on deception, financial manipulation, and military intimidation. Democracy is framed as a smokescreen for imperial control, and the U.S. is accused of deliberately sabotaging other nations through "easy money" and debt traps.
  
> 
  
> Glorification of Chinese leadership
  
> Xi Jinping is described as "our dear fearless leader" trying to save China from the predatory Western financial system. This type of language is straight out of CCP propaganda and reinforces the narrative of Xi as the heroic guardian of Chinese sovereignty and civilization.
  
> 
  
> Historical revisionism and economic inversion
  
> The lecture claims that the U.S. gave away military and economic secrets to China intentionally, portraying China as a passive recipient rather than an active participant in intellectual property theft, espionage, and economic strategy. This shifts blame away from documented behavior and creates a false narrative of betrayal.
  
> 
  
> Portrayal of authoritarian states as rational and heroic
  
> Putin is called a genius, North Korea is framed as a strategic ally, and Iran is presented as a victim of American aggression. These are standard themes in pro-CCP or anti-Western messaging, where authoritarian regimes are cast as legitimate challengers to Western domination.
  
> 
  
> Promotion of a multipolar anti-Western future
  
> The lecture suggests that China, Russia, and Iran form a righteous front against the declining American empire. This aligns with the CCP's goal of undermining the current U.S.-led world order and promoting an alternative international structure centered on authoritarian cooperation.
  
> 
  
> Omission of internal abuses
  
> There is no mention of China's internal censorship, surveillance, mass incarceration of Uyghurs, or political repression. These critical realities are excluded entirely, which is a key feature of disinformation designed to sanitize authoritarian regimes while demonizing Western ones.
  
> 
  
> Anti-capitalist, anti-democratic undercurrent
  
> While disclaiming conspiracy theory, the speaker recycles common conspiracies about central banks controlling world events. These ideas blur economic reality with populist suspicion, appealing to resentment while distracting from systemic issues in authoritarian regimes.
  
> 
  
> Emotional manipulation
  
> The speaker ends on an uplifting note about love and imagination, aiming to inspire students and cast the entire narrative as a call to human greatness. This tactic softens the radical anti-Westernism by cloaking it in the language of universal values.
  
> 
  
> Overwhelming narrative volume
  
> The lecture floods the listener with names, dates, and economic terms to create an illusion of academic credibility. This is a common tactic in propaganda: overwhelm the audience so they accept the speaker’s conclusions without critically engaging the individual claims.
  
> 
  
> Final judgment
  
> Whether state-sponsored or not, this lecture aligns closely with CCP messaging goals. It delegitimizes the United States, lionizes authoritarian leaders, and frames China as both a victim and a redeemer on the world stage. This is textbook narrative warfare—sophisticated, seemingly academic, but designed to shift allegiance, especially among globally-minded youth.
  
> 
  
> The lecture is not just biased; it functions as psychological and ideological recruitment into a worldview that favors authoritarian resistance to Western influence. It fits the mold of what the CCP calls united front work: using unofficial voices to further official aims.

I was pleasantly surprised to see "Tragedy and Hope" by Carroll Quigley referred to. 
This book has been derided for not having footnotes or references. The reason for this absence is that this book was not intended for the general public. In fact, for a time it was suppressed. 
I have a first edition of this book. Its treatment of World War II and its structure and financing alone make it required reading.
I recommend we also take his recommendation to check out: 
"The Open Society and its Enemies", volumes I and II by Karl Popper.

Just to clarify, the reason easy money destroys a country is because it increases the money supply, which means more money is chasing the same supply of goods, which means that their prices rise (because effectively, each unit of currency represents less value). This makes people's paychecks worth less. They can try to get raises to counter this, but in practice raises overall do not keep up with the rate of price increases. Thus the bulk of the population grows poorer, and cannot afford more than the essentials. It's why inflation, in the long run, kills economies, while deflation makes economies robust & healthy (see: USA in the 19th century, deflation for decades, massive economic growth; all modern economists claim deflation hurts economies but history objectively proves them wrong; economists are con artists and liars).

Just a slight correction on something you said in the beginning of your lecture. In Calvinism, or Protestantism in general, it wasn't so much that becoming rich would make you go to heaven. It was much more so that enjoiying the fruits of your labor was considered sinful and something of the devil, since this life in the flesh was a product of the Fall from Eden after Adam and Eve commited the first sin. In the 16th and 17th century the Dutch were becoming more and more succesful in international trade. And also, after 80 years of war, they defeated the Spanish (a selfconsciously catholic empire) in 1648, which boosted a distinctly protestant nationalism in the Netherlands. So you had selfconsciously protestant succesful traders emerging for the first time in history. If you are, morally, not allowed to enjoy the fruits of your succesful labor, the only thing you can do, is invest your profits in your business. This made protestant business owners very welthy and naturally tended to expanding their business, since investing was the only option. This was, I believe, also the thesis of Weber, early in the 20th century, to explain the rise of capitalism in protestant Europe. I am Dutch-Spanish by the way. Great channel!

Original NATO 
The Delian League was an alliance of ancient Greek city-states, formed in 478 BCE after the Greco-Persian Wars to continue fighting the Persian Empire. Led by Athens, the League's treasury was initially on the sacred island of Delos, but the alliance gradually transformed into an Athenian empire due to Athens' growing dominance and the use of member states' tribute
 for non-defense purposes. The League eventually collapsed after being defeated by Sparta and its allies in the Peloponnesian War (431–404 BCE).

### **@ConfuciusSaysBoo**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=_gH4PvIni5E&lc=UgydTcCDxmNyJyXEkjt4AaABAg)

The Federal Reserve expands the monetary supply by adjusting interest rates to allow the private banking industry to expand line of credit lending to large corporations, this effectively increases the money supply. The Federal Reserve works with the Department of Treasury to finance excess (deficit) spending of the US Government through the US Treasury Bond market..This is the second way to expand the money supply. That is the core function of the Federal Reserve.... It gets more complicated when you dig in and look at its modern history of increasing and decreasing its balance sheet's ownership of US Treasury Notes. That happens when the Fed needs to squeeze inflation and/or the money supply or cannot find investors for the US Treasury Notes. In addition, the Fed has started propping up the junk bond market by interceding into that market when it looks to go belly up...minor issue, but demonstrates the instructor's proposition of the Fed's control over US and to some extent global finances.

Oldest Nations and Civilizations (Approximate Order of Establishment) 

Iran (Persia) (c. 3200 BCE): The Elamite kingdom and subsequent Persian empires represent one of the longest continuous civilizations.

Egypt (c. 3100 BCE): Unified under King Menes, leading to the First Dynasty, with a history spanning over 5,000 years.

Vietnam (c. 2879 BCE): Traces its origins to the Hồng Bàng dynasty.

Armenia (c. 2492 BCE): Traditionally founded by Hayk, it is one of the oldest recorded state entities.

China (c. 2070 BCE): Originating with the Xia Dynasty, marking the beginning of Chinese civilization.

India (c. 2000 BCE): Rooted in the Indus Valley Civilization, one of the world's earliest urban cultures.

Georgia (c. 1300 BCE): Early state formations date back to the Bronze Age.

Ethiopia (c. 980 BCE): The Dʿmt kingdom established one of the earliest states in East Africa.

Greece (c. 800–3000 BCE): While Mycenaean/Minoan cultures date earlier (3000 BCE), the Hellenic city-states began defining Greek civilization around 800 BCE.

Japan (c. 660 BCE): Traditionally founded by Emperor Jimmu.

Jiang Xueqin’s profile makes a CCP connection plausible. He is a Yale-educated intellectual who built his career in Beijing’s elite schools such as Peking University High School International Division and Tsinghua University High School. These institutions are not independent; they are governed by Party committees that control hiring, promotions, and curricula. Advancement to senior roles in such environments requires political reliability and at least tacit acceptance of Party oversight.

His public commentary also echoes CCP narratives. In his YouTube lectures he repeatedly stresses that the West is decadent, fragmented, and doomed to collapse — themes central to Chinese state media messaging. Whether this is conscious propaganda or personal conviction, the effect is the same: reinforcing Beijing’s preferred worldview to a global audience.

Finally, Jiang has positioned himself as a bridge figure — Western-educated, fluent in elite Chinese institutions, and speaking to English-language audiences. Such figures are strategically useful for the CCP’s broader effort to shape international discourse.

There is no open evidence that Jiang is a Party member or operative. But given his career within CCP-controlled structures, his rhetorical alignment, and his global positioning, a CCP link remains a distinct possibility.

In fact the dollars to buy those bonds has already been spent into existence before they are sold. The US settles over 140 trillion in bonds every year. The reason other countries get into debt trouble is because they borrow in foreign currencies and have to get that foreign currency in order to pay it back. For example Argentina is in debt with US Dollars. It can’t create dollars and it’s currency isn’t in high enough demand for it to sell their currency for Dollars.
